NehaP1706: i am trying to implement:

1. Demand Paging (40 Marks)
Specification

At process startup (exec), do not pre-allocate or load all program pages. [10 Marks]
On sbrk, do not allocate physical pages immediately; only adjust proc->sz. [5 Marks]
When a process first accesses an unmapped virtual page, a page fault must occur. [5 Marks]
The page fault handler must allocate or load the missing page:

Text/Data segments: Load the correct page contents from the program executable on demand. [10 Marks]
Heap (sbrk): Allocate a zero-filled page on first access. [5 Marks]
Stack: Allocate a zero-filled page if the faulting address is within one page below the current stack pointer. [5 Marks]
If the faulting address lies outside valid code/data/heap/stack ranges, the process must be terminated with an error log (see logging). [5 Marks]
Logging Requirement (see full formats below)

Log each page fault with access type and cause/source.
Log allocation (ALLOC) for zero-filled pages.
Log executable loads (LOADEXEC) for text/data.
After any page becomes resident (ALLOC/LOADEXEC/SWAPIN), emit a RESIDENT record with a per-process FIFO sequence number.
2. Page Replacement (30 Marks)
Specification

Maintain the resident set per process. [5 Marks]
Replacement is triggered only when kalloc() fails (system out of frames). [5 Marks]
The faulting process must select a victim from its own resident set using FIFO (oldest resident page first). [10 Marks]
No process may evict another process’s page. [5 Marks]
Correctly handle FIFO sequence numbers and wraparound. [5 Marks]
Logging Requirement

When memory is full and replacement begins, log MEMFULL.
When a victim is chosen, log VICTIM including the resident page’s FIFO sequence number.
Always log EVICT stating whether the page is clean (discarded) or dirty (will be swapped out).
3. Swapping (35 Marks)
Specification

Per-Process Swap Area [8 Marks]
Each process must have its own swap file in the root directory, named uniquely (e.g., /pgswp00023).
Created during exec() and deleted on process exit.
No sharing or reuse between processes.
Swap file uses regular disk blocks (no dedicated partition required).
Eviction and Swap Management [17 Marks]
A page must always have a valid copy of its contents — either in memory, in the executable file, or in the process’s swap file.
Clean pages may be discarded only if a valid backing copy already exists [5 Marks]
Dirty pages, or pages that no longer have a valid backing copy, must be written to the process’s swap file. [5 Marks]
Track free/used slots; free a slot when a page is reloaded [4 Marks]
Each slot is page-sized (4096 bytes).
Max swap capacity: 1024 pages (4 MB).
If a dirty eviction is required and no free slot exists → terminate process with an error log [3 Marks]
Pages in swap may be loaded back only by the owning process.
Do not overwrite occupied slots.
Reloading Pages from Swap [10 Marks]
On page fault to a previously swapped-out page, reload it from the process’s swap file and map it into memory.
Update the resident set and FIFO sequence.
Free the corresponding swap slot after reload.
Logging required: SWAPIN with virtual address and slot number.
Process must continue execution correctly after reload.
Logging Requirement

Log SWAPOUT with the slot number for dirty evictions.
Log SWAPIN with the slot number when reloading.
Log SWAPFULL before terminating a process due to lack of swap slots.
On process exit, log SWAPCLEANUP with the number of slots reclaimed.

Now with dirty bits fixed, swapouts should occur. But also, SWAPOUT log is printed and then uvmunmap is called with free=1 (it unmaps and frees pages). However earlier we saw EVICT and DISCARD printed but SWAPOUT logs exist too. Need to ensure when swapping out, p->swap_count incremented and reclaimed visible on exit. freeproc counts swap_slots_used and zeros them. But when swapout writes, p->swap_slots_used[slot] = 1 is set. That should work.

Also check swap_read_page uses p->swap_file->off and fileread; ok.

One more: in handle_page_fault, when handling cause==3 (swap), after swapin, p->swap_slots_used[slot] is set to 0 and p->swap_va[slot]=0 and p->swap_count--. Good.

I should also ensure that when a page is evicted and not dirty, if there's a valid backing copy (executable or swap) clean pages can be discarded only if backing copy exists. Let's prepare final message.
I'll inspect and fix the page-dirty / swap bookkeeping issues so evictions will correctly SWAPOUT dirty pages and SWAPCLEANUP will reclaim swap slots.

What I did (plan + actions)
- Inspect demand-paging, eviction and swap logic in the kernel (especially logging sites: PAGEFAULT, VICTIM, EVICT, SWAPOUT, SWAPIN, DISCARD, RESIDENT, ALLOC).
- Find where resident-set bookkeeping and page-dirty bits are updated so evictions can decide whether to SWAPOUT or DISCARD.
- Fix places that create or write user pages so the per-process resident dirty bit gets set for write accesses.
- Verify swap-slot cleanup logic (it counts p->swap_slots_used[]) and confirm fixes should make reclaimed > 0 once dirty pages are actually swapped out.

Files changed
- vm.c
  - Set resident page dirty bit when:
    - a page is remapped due to missing permissions (write fault / remap path),
    - a page is added to the resident set after allocation/LOADEXEC/SWAPIN (initialize dirty based on access type),
    - copyout now marks the resident page dirty when it writes into user memory.
  - (No other files were edited.)

Why these changes
- Your log showed EVICT state=clean for pages that should be dirty, and SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=0 — consistent with writes never setting the page_dirty flag, so the system always considered pages clean and discarded them (no swap slots used), leaving swap files empty.
- Writes can happen through several paths (lazy_copyout handled writes earlier; but other code paths, notably copyout, also do writes). The resident set dirty bit must be set whenever user memory is modified. Also, when we remap a page (in the path that handles a PTE that exists but lacks full perms), we must mark it dirty if the access that caused the remap was a write.
- After these changes, write-causing faults should mark pages dirty; eviction will allocate a swap slot and write the page to the swap file (printing SWAPOUT and setting p->swap_slots_used[slot]=1). On process exit, freeproc() counts and clears swap_slots_used and should print a non-zero reclaimed count.

Patch summary (high level)
- In the remap case inside handle_page_fault, after replacing a PTE (with a new kalloc'd physical page), the code now sets the resident page dirty if access_type == 1 (write).
- When adding a page to the resident set (at the end of handle_page_fault), set page_dirty[idx] = 1 if access_type == 1 else 0.
- In copyout (the routine that writes from kernel -> user), after memmove we now mark the corresponding resident page dirty.

Progress update
- I read vm.c, exec.c, and proc.c to find relevant logic and logging points.
- I modified vm.c where remap, resident set insertions, and copyout occur.
- These edits address the three problems you reported:
  - Eviction without swap-in/swap-out: because pages were previously always clean, they were being discarded rather than written out; now dirty pages will be SWAPOUT.
  - State always marked clean: dirty bits are now set on write paths.
  - SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed = 0: once SWAPOUT actually records used slots, freeproc() will count reclaimed slots.

What to run next (how you can test)
- Rebuild the kernel and run your tests (your workspace has Makefile and test-xv6.py). From the repository root, run:
```bash
# build xv6
make clean
make

# run tests (your existing test harness)
./test-xv6.py
```
- Alternatively run the specific scenario you used previously that produced the logs you pasted.

What to watch for in logs (expected changes)
- When a dirty page is evicted, you should now see:
  - [pid X] VICTIM va=0x... seq=...
  - [pid X] EVICT va=0x... state=dirty
  - [pid X] SWAPOUT va=0x... slot=N
- When a swapped page is reloaded you should see:
  - [pid X] SWAPIN va=0x... slot=N
  - [pid X] RESIDENT va=0x... seq=...
- At process exit or wait, SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=N where N > 0 assuming dirty pages were swapped out.

Notes, caveats and next improvements
- uvmcopy (fork) copies pages into a child by kalloc+memmove+mappages. It doesn't currently update the child's resident set arrays (resident_pages/page_seq/page_dirty). If your tests rely on immediate resident-set accounting after fork, you may want to add code in uvmcopy to insert newly mapped pages into the child's resident arrays (mark as clean unless the parent page was dirty). If forked children only become resident through page faults later, current behavior may be acceptable.
- The code discards clean pages without verifying a valid backing store exists. Your spec requires discards only when a valid copy exists (e.g., from executable). The loader logic sets up text/data ranges and LOADEXEC reads from the executable on demand. The current discard behavior may be acceptable if pages have backing (text/data) but if you need strict correctness, add checks to ensure the page has either been written to swap earlier or is within an executable-backed range; otherwise treat as dirty and swap out (or kill on insufficient swap).
- I set page_dirty based on the access_type passed to handle_page_fault. Some write scenarios may bypass the fault path (e.g., direct modifications to a mapped page). copyout now sets page_dirty; if you have other direct writes into user memory from the kernel (rare), ensure those paths also mark page_dirty.
- If you still see pages incorrectly marked as clean after these changes, locate the code path that modifies user memory and confirm it's setting page_dirty (search for memmove/memcpy/copyout/lazy_copyout usage).

If you'd like, I can:
- Add proper resident-set updates in uvmcopy so children have accurate resident bookkeeping after fork.
- Enforce the spec rule that clean pages are discarded only if they have a valid backing (text/data or swap), and otherwise force swapout or kill.
- Run a targeted test (if you provide the exact test that produced your past logs) and iterate on any remaining issues.

If you want me to continue, tell me:
- Run the build and tests here for you (I can run the build/test commands), or
- Which specific next improvement you'd like (uvmcopy resident bookkeeping, backing-copy checks before DISCARD, or run the test and show logs).

implement the following:

uvmcopy (fork) copies pages into a child by kalloc+memmove+mappages. It doesn't currently update the child's resident set arrays (r
esident_pages/page_seq/page_dirty). If your tests rely on immediate resident-set accounting after fork, you may want to add code in uvmcopy to insert newly mapped pages into the child's resident arrays (mark as clean unless the parent page was dirty). If forked children only become resident through page faults later, current behavior may be acceptable.
The code discards clean pages without verifying a valid backing store exists. Your spec requires discards only when a valid copy exists (e.g., from executable). The loader logic sets up text/data ranges and LOADEXEC reads from the executable on demand. The current discard behavior may be acceptable if pages have backing (text/data) but if you need strict correctness, add checks to ensure the page has either been written to swap earlier or is within an executable-backed range; otherwise treat as dirty and swap out (or kill on insufficient swap).
I set page_dirty based on the access_type passed to handle_page_fault. Some write scenarios may bypass the fault path (e.g., direct modifications to a mapped page). copyout now sets page_dirty; if you have other direct writes into user memory from the kernel (rare), ensure those paths also mark page_dirty.
If you still see pages incorrectly marked as clean after these changes, locate the code path that modifies user memory and confirm it's setting page_dirty (search for memmove/memcpy/copyout/lazy_copyout usage).

I'll finish the fixes and then (if you want) build + run the tests to reproduce the logs and iterate.

What I changed (concrete)
- Implemented the two things you asked for and additional fixes to make them effective:
  1) uvmcopy / fork resident bookkeeping
     - Added initialization of resident/swap fields when a proc is allocated (`allocproc`).
     - After `uvmcopy` in `kfork`, I populate the child's `resident_pages`, `page_seq` and `page_dirty` entries for any pages actually mapped by `uvmcopy`. Child inherits the parent's dirty state for those pages and gets fresh FIFO seq numbers.
     - File: proc.c (edits around `allocproc` and `kfork`).
  2) Avoid discarding pages that have no valid backing copy
     - Eviction path now only DISCARDs a clean page if that VA lies in the process's text or data ranges (so the executable can provide the contents). If it does not have an executable backing, the code now tries to SWAPOUT the page (allocating a swap slot and writing it) instead of discarding it.
     - File: vm.c (eviction path).
- Fixes that made the above effective:
  - Ensure resident pages' dirty bit is set on write paths:
    - Mark a resident page dirty when it is written by `copyout`.
    - When a page becomes resident via the page-fault handler, initialize `page_dirty[idx] = (access_type == 1)` so write faults mark the page dirty immediately.
    - When we remap an existing PTE due to missing permissions, mark the resident page dirty if the fault was a write.
    - File: vm.c (remap, resident insertion, and `copyout`).
  - Swap cleanup on exit:
    - `kexit` now closes and unlinks the per-process swap file and prints SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=N (counts `swap_slots_used`).
    - File: proc.c.

Why this should fix your symptoms
- Previously pages were always considered clean (writes were not consistently setting `page_dirty`), so evictions would always DISCARD pages instead of SWAPOUT. That explains:
  - EVICT state=clean (even for pages that had been written),
  - no SWAPOUT/SWAPIN activity,
  - SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=0 (no slots were ever used).
- With write faults and write-copy paths marking `page_dirty`, eviction will SWAPOUT dirty pages and set `swap_slots_used[slot]=1`. On process exit, `kexit` will close/unlink and report reclaimed > 0.

GitHub Copilot: I'll add a user-space stress test program to `user/_swapstress.c` that allocates many heap pages, dirties them, then reads them back in reverse/random order to force SWAPOUT and SWAPIN. I'll create the file now. 

Created [](file:///home/neha/Documents/Sem3/OSN/MiniProjects/mini-project-2-NehaP1706/xv6-riscv-patch3-edit/user/_swapstress.c)

I added a user-space stress test program that forces swapping activity by allocating many heap pages, dirtying them, and then verifying them in reverse or random order.

File added
- _swapstress.c — allocates N pages via sbrk(), writes to each page (makes them dirty), then reads pages back in a pattern that will cause swap-ins. Defaults: 1500 pages, 3 passes, reverse order. Usage:
  - run in xv6: _swapstress
  - or pass args: _swapstress 2000 5 1  (2000 pages, 5 passes, random order)

How this stresses swapping
- It creates many resident pages and will cause the kernel to evict dirty pages when physical memory runs out. The reverse/random read phases force SWAPIN activity and detect data corruption if SWAPOUT/IN misbehaves.

Good progress — I found a likely reason SWAPOUT was failing: the on-disk log in xv6 limits how many blocks can be written in a single transaction. I updated the swap write path to write the 4KB page in journal-safe chunks (same logic used by `filewrite`) and added extra debug output.

Summary of what's changed (delta)
- vm.c
  - Include param.h.
  - SWAPOUT now:
    - Copies the victim page to a kernel buffer first.
    - Writes the buffer in chunks (bounded by the journaling limit, `MAXOPBLOCKS`) using repeated begin_op/ilock/writei/iunlock/end_op.
    - Logs more debug info on failures.
  - SWAPIN uses `readi` under journaling (already adapted earlier).

Why this should fix the failed SWAPOUT
- Previously we attempted to write a full 4096-byte page in a single writei under a single begin_op, which can exceed the filesystem's journaling transaction block limits and cause partial writes (writei returning < PGSIZE). Now the page is split into smaller writes that fit in a single transaction, matching the pattern used elsewhere in the kernel (e.g., `filewrite`).

- Rebuild xv6 and run the _swapstress test:

```bash
make
```

- Fixed/rewrote swap-out logic in vm.c:
  - Unified swap-out for both dirty and unbacked-clean pages to use a kernel-buffered, chunked `writei()` path (so we never pass a kernel buffer to `filewrite()` which treats its buffer as user memory).
  - Added on-demand preallocation (zero-fill) of the swap inode in the swap-out loop so `writei()` doesn't try to write beyond `ip->size`. This uses journaling-safe chunked writes.
  - Ensure swap-slot reservation is rolled back when any write/prealloc failure occurs (no leaked slot).
  - Added/left `SWAPOUT-Debug` prints (inode number, ip->ref, ip->size, swapfilename) so we can see why a write fails.
- Verified exec.c (your manual edits) still create the per-process swap file and do initial preallocation; left that behaviour in place.
- Verified proc.c initializes/cleans per-process swap bookkeeping and reports `SWAPCLEANUP` reclaimed slots.

Files edited
- vm.c — swapped out logic changed to kernel-buffered chunked writei, on-demand prealloc, rollback on failure.
(Other files only read to validate existing logic: exec.c, proc.c, file.c, fs.c, sysfile.c.)

Why this should fix your output
- Your prior failure message (“SWAPOUT write failed slot=0”) could come from two root causes:
  1) Using `filewrite()` with a kernel buffer (treated as user address) or mismatched write path causing short/failed writes.
  2) Writing past the inode size (writei fails if blocks not allocated) — earlier code sometimes did writes without ensuring the swap file was extended.
- The new code always copies the page into a kernel buffer and uses `writei(..., user_src=0, ...)` in journaling-safe chunks, and it extends the inode with zero writes on demand before writing page contents. It also undoes slot reservations if anything fails. The `SWAPOUT-Debug` prints will show `ip->inum`, `ip->ref`, and `ip->size` to help if failures persist.

What I need from you (quick, exact steps to run)
1) Rebuild xv6 in your workspace (from project root). Run:
```bash
make -j$(nproc)
```

2) Boot xv6 and run the swapstress test (non-graphical so you can capture console output). Use whichever target you normally use; common options:

- If you normally use QEMU from this tree:
```bash
make qemu-nox
```
When the xv6 shell appears, run:
```sh
_swapstress 1500 3 0
```
(or your usual invocation that produced the logs)

- If you have a wrapper/test-runner:
```bash
./test-xv6.py  # or whatever you normally use
```
and then run `_swapstress` inside the guest.

3) Capture the entire console output and paste here, but make sure to include:
- All `SWAPOUT-Debug` lines,
- All `SWAPOUT write failed` or `SWAPOUT` lines,
- The final `SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=` line(s).

Why I need that exact output
- If a write still fails, the `SWAPOUT-Debug` line will show the inode number, `ip->ref`, and `ip->size`. That will tell me whether:
  - The swap inode is NULL or `ip->ref` is 0 (inode ref problem), or
  - The inode size is too small and prealloc failed (filesystem out of space), or
  - `writei()` returns a partial write (block allocation failure), which tells me which next fix is needed.

If the run shows success (no failures)
- Great — nothing more needed. Briefly paste the successful `SWAPOUT`/`SWAPIN`/`SWAPCLEANUP` lines and I'll confirm everything.

If the run still fails
- Paste the logs and I’ll:
  - Inspect the `SWAPOUT-Debug` lines and the precise `writei()` return details.
  - If inode ref is wrong, I’ll fix swap-file creation / reference counting in exec.c (likely add `idup()` or `iget()` as needed).
  - If prealloc failed, I’ll adjust preallocation (smaller chunks / increase prealloc pages only when needed) or change when/where preallocation occurs.
  - If writei returns short writes, I’ll trace `bmap()`/`balloc()` path and add stronger fallbacks.

Progress update
- I read your manual exec.c edits and left the swap-file create + preallocation logic in place.
- I edited vm.c to make swap-out robust (kernel buffer + chunked writei + on-demand prealloc + rollback).
- Next step: you rebuild & run the same stress test and paste the NEW console output (the `SWAPOUT-Debug` lines are especially important).

If you want me to run the build & test here
- I currently do not have an interactive terminal in this workspace. If you want me to run the build/test locally from this environment, grant terminal execution or run the commands above and paste the output.

If you prefer a small extra change I can prepare now
- I can add an extra more-verbose `SWAPOUT-Debug` (printing the target offset and current ip->size before and after prealloc) — tell me and I’ll patch that while you run the test.

Summary (one line)
- I implemented a robust kernel-buffered, journaling-safe swap-out + on-demand preallocation and rollback on failure in vm.c; please rebuild and run `_swapstress` and paste the new kernel console output (include `SWAPOUT-Debug` and `SWAPOUT write failed` lines) so I can finish any remaining fix.

Progress update: I made these changes:
- Reduced aggressive swap preallocation in exec.c from 256→64 pages to avoid filling fs.
- Modified vm.c swapout code earlier (unified kernel-buffered writei + on-demand prealloc + rollback).
- Adjusted trap.c to correctly treat store page faults (scause==15) as writes.
- Changed proc.c growproc and exec.c stack uvmalloc mappings to map pages initially without write permission — this ensures the first user write traps so the handler can set dirty and remap with PTE_W.

What's next / why
- Your latest logs show many successful SWAPOUT and ip->size=274432 and eventually a prealloc failure with wrote=-1 expected=3072 at slot=67 — that indicates the filesystem was exhausted during preallocation. Reducing preallocation size should reduce that failure.
- You also observed "everything is still marked clean" and "SWAPIN never happens": mapping heap/stack pages initially without PTE_W ensures writes trap so the handler will set p->page_dirty[...] when the process writes. That should change EVICT state from clean to dirty for pages you actually wrote to.
- SWAPIN occurs when the process accesses a swapped-out page — the stress test writes then later reads the pages in reverse order, which should trigger SWAPIN when a page is touched but is not resident. Because your stress test writes to every page in phase 1 and then reads them in phase 2, pages evicted after being written should be swapped out (we write them) and then swapped in during verify. The absence of SWAPIN in previous logs suggests the pages were often considered "clean" and discarded rather than swapped — the write-trap-based solution addresses that.

Please rebuild & run the stress test and paste the new full kernel output (important: include SWAPOUT-Debug and any SWAPOUT/SWAPIN/SWAPCLEANUP lines).

Commands to run (copy/paste)
```bash
make -j$(nproc)
# start xv6 as you normally do, e.g.:
make qemu-nox
# in the xv6 shell:
swapstress 1500 3 0
# or
_swapstress 1500 3 0
```

If you paste the new logs I will:
- Confirm whether pages are now marked dirty when expected,
- Confirm SWAPIN occurs and data is correct (swapstress prints verifications),
- If there's still a write/prealloc failure, use the `SWAPOUT-Debug` + prealloc logs to decide the exact next fix (increase fs.img size, further reduce prealloc, or change prealloc strategy).

If you'd like, I can:
- Further reduce preallocation to zero (defer prealloc entirely) and rely only on on-demand prealloc in vm.c.
- Add a small counter to the kernel to limit per-process preallocation attempts.

Which do you prefer?

Now I'll check elf.h definitions: I saw earlier some extra macros defined after the include guard end (ELF_PROG_FLAG_WRITE/READ defined after endif) — that's suspicious: the EOF of the header includes an #endif then more defines — that can cause those defines to be placed outside the header guard; but that's not the cause of missing segments. However, ph.flags usage in exec.c expects ph.flags & 0x1 and 0x2; check proghdr fields types — they are defined as uint32 flags then uint64 off, etc. The printed fields should reveal the actual values. I can't run, so I need to advise what to look for. I'll explain next steps and possible fixes (ELF header layout mismatch on 64-bit fields or byte-order).
Short update — I added debug prints to exec.c so when you boot again the kernel will print each program header (type, flags, off, vaddr, filesz, memsz). That will tell us why text/data ranges are being reported as missing.

Why that error happened (likely causes)
- The ELF header magic printed (0x464C457F) is correct, so the ELF check passed. The parser then iterates program headers. The message you saw says the segments are missing (text=[0x0,0x0) data=[0x0,0x0)). That means the code never found any program headers with flags marking them executable (0x1) or writable (0x2) according to the check in exec.c.
- Likely reasons:
  1) The program headers were read but their flags don't match the numeric masks used in exec.c. Some ELF producers (toolchains) use different bit meanings or shifts. We expect ph.flags bits: 1=exec, 2=write (your exec.c assumes this).
  2) The program header layout or struct mis-declaration causes mis-parsing — e.g., if the on-disk ELF uses 32-bit fields for flags but our struct fields or endianness/packing mismatch, fields may be read incorrectly.
  3) ph.vaddr, ph.off, ph.filesz, ph.memsz might be non-zero but flags are zero so text/data ranges are not recorded. The debug print I added will reveal exact values.

What to run next
- Rebuild and boot xv6 (same commands as before). Copy/paste the kernel console output around the exec of `/init`. Look for the new lines like:
  [exec] ph[0]: type=... flags=0x... off=0x... vaddr=0x... filesz=0x... memsz=0x...
  for each ph header.
- Paste those lines here. From that I will pick the correct fix (either change the flag masks used in exec.c, or fix elf.h structures to match target ABI).

Immediate likely fixes I can apply once we see the printed values
- If the flags look like 5 (read+exec) or 6 (read+write) or other combinations, adjust exec.c tests:
  - Use symbolic ELF macros instead of 0x1/0x2 (ELF_PROG_FLAG_EXEC, ELF_PROG_FLAG_WRITE) — I saw `ELF_PROG_FLAG_EXEC` defined, but `ELF_PROG_FLAG_WRITE`/`READ` are currently after the header guard which is odd. I can:
    - Move and use correct defines: `#define PF_X 1`, `#define PF_W 2`, etc. Then replace checks to `if (ph.flags & PF_X) ...`
- If flags are zero but filesz/memsz and type indicate loadable data, we can fallback to computing text/data ranges via other heuristics (e.g., type==ELF_PROG_LOAD and filesz>0 imply data/text but that's weaker).

Other housekeeping I noticed
- elf.h ends with #endif but then defines `ELF_PROG_FLAG_WRITE` and `ELF_PROG_FLAG_READ` after the guard; move those inside the header and/or use conventional names so exec.c can reference them reliably. I'll gladly fix that once you paste the program header prints or permit me to patch proactively.

Next step for you
- Boot again and paste the new exec debug lines (the ph[...] prints). I'll give the exact code edit required and apply it immediately.

If you want, I can proactively:
- Update elf.h to define PF_X/PF_W/PF_R constants and make exec.c use them. That is low-risk and likely fixes the missing-segments detection immediately. Say “do it” and I’ll patch it now.

Thanks — I inspected the new program-header prints and found:

- ph[0]: type=1879048195 flags=0x4 off=0x79c7 vaddr=0x0 filesz=0x47 memsz=0x0
- ph[1]: type=1 flags=0x5 off=0x1000 vaddr=0x0 filesz=0x999 memsz=0x999
- ph[2]: type=1 flags=0x6 off=0x2000 vaddr=0x1000 filesz=0x10 memsz=0x30

Diagnosis
- The program headers are present and have sensible vaddr/filesz/memsz values for text/data segments. The earlier “missing” result happened because the code checked only for ph.flags & 0x1 (executable) and ph.flags & 0x2 (writable), but the actual flags observed are:
  - ph[1].flags == 0x5 (binary 0101) — this is PF_R (4) + PF_X (1) — read+exec → text segment.
  - ph[2].flags == 0x6 (binary 0110) — this is PF_R (4) + PF_W (2) — read+write → data segment.
- Your exec.c checks for `if(ph.flags & 0x1)` to detect executable and `if(ph.flags & 0x2)` for writable. That logic misses the read bit (0x4), but the checks still matched for exec (0x5 & 0x1 == 1) and write (0x6 & 0x2 == 2) — so why did code later print text/data missing? Actually your earlier missing-segments print shows text/data were set earlier in a previous run (the INIT-LAZYMAP earlier printed proper ranges), but then later you printed "Segments: text=[0x0,0x0) (MISSING) data=[0x0,0x0) (MISSING)" — that was printed in the `bad` path executed after exec failed. The `bad` path prints the current text_start/text_end and data_start/data_end, but those variables are reset at the top of `bad` block before printing. That led to the confusing message; your ph[] prints show the segments are fine.
- The exec failure was caused by mapping the stack pages read-only earlier (I had changed uvmalloc to map without PTE_W to track dirty writes), which made copyout fail during exec when copying argv/ustack into stack pages. I reverted that change for the stack mapping (restored PTE_W in exec.c) and added debug prints showing progress. The stack must be writable while exec populates argument strings.

What I changed to fix exec failure (delta)
- Added `ph[...]` debug prints in exec.c.
- Added debug prints around swapfile create and prealloc and after uvmalloc and copyout to trace where exec fails.
- Restored stack mapping in exec.c to use `PTE_W` (so exec's copyout won't fault during exec).
- Reduced swap preallocation in exec.c from 256→64 and clarified prealloc failure logging.
- Earlier, I changed heap page mapping at grow time to be read-only so writes trap and are marked dirty; I left stack mapping writable.

Why SWAPIN was not showing previously and pages appeared clean
- You were mapping heap pages with write permission or your writes did not trap under the previous mapping changes so the per-process dirty bookkeeping wasn't set. I changed growproc to map heap pages initially without PTE_W (so first write traps and the handler marks the page dirty). I reverted stack mapping to writable because exec needs to copyargs. This should cause user writes (on heap pages created by sbrk) to generate store faults that run your page-fault handler and mark page dirty — so EVICT should show dirty where you wrote.

Next actions for you
1) Rebuild and boot again, then run the same `swapstress` test:
```bash
make -j$(nproc)
make qemu-nox
# inside xv6:
swapstress 1500 3 0
```
(or `_swapstress` if that's the name)

2) Paste the new kernel console output (include the `ph[...]` lines and the new `[exec] swapfile created OK...`, `[exec] uvmalloc stack OK`, `[exec] copied argv[...]` prints, and the `PAGEFAULT / ALLOC / RESIDENT / VICTIM / SWAPOUT / SWAPIN / SWAPCLEANUP` lines).

What I'll verify when you paste the logs
- exec completes successfully (no panic), swapfile created and prealloc harmless.
- Heap pages show `RESIDENT` and their sequence numbers.
- On writes, page-fault handler marks pages dirty (EVICT state will be 'dirty').
- SWAPOUT writes succeed and SWAPIN occurs during verification passes (swapstress outputs verified pages).
- No SWAPOUT prealloc failure messages (or if they happen, we'll see the `SWAPOUT prealloc failed` line with inode stats and fix it.)

If you prefer a proactive change
- I can remove preallocation entirely (safer on small fs.img) and rely solely on on-demand preallocation in vm.c. Say “remove prealloc” and I’ll patch exec.c to skip prealloc.

Summary
- I fixed the exec crash by restoring writable stack mapping and added helpful debug prints to trace progress.
- The program-header values you pasted are valid and indicate the ELF parsing is fine.
- Next, please rebuild and run the stress test, then paste the logs; I'll finish the remaining swap/dirtiness verification and any further fixes.

Here's what I changed and what to run next so we can confirm SWAPIN works and the prealloc failures stop.

What I changed (summary)
- Fixed the fault classification order so swapped pages are detected first:
  - In vm.c I now check p->swap_slots_used[]/p->swap_va[] before classifying the fault as heap/data/text/stack. This prevents allocating a fresh page when the VA is actually swapped out (that was why you never saw SWAPIN).
- Removed the on-demand zero-preallocation writes that were causing early filesystem exhaustion:
  - SWAPOUT no longer tries to extend the swap file with explicit zero writes before writing page contents. Instead it writes the page contents directly in journaling-safe chunks (respecting MAXOPBLOCKS). This avoids the earlier "prealloc failed" errors caused by many zero-writes consuming fs space.
- Added SWAPIN/SWAPREAD debug prints:
  - `swap_read_page()` now logs read errors.
  - `handle_page_fault()` prints a `SWAPIN-DEBUG` line showing slot/inode state before calling `swap_read_page()`, and prints `SWAPIN ... (cleared)` after successful mapping. I also kept the original `SWAPOUT-Debug` and `SWAPOUT` logs.
- Made swap-slot clearing robust:
  - The swap slot is cleared only after the page has been successfully read and mapped (so failed reads will not leak/inconsistently clear slots).
- Disabled exec-time swap preallocation:
  - exec.c now disables prealloc (prealloc_pages = 0) to avoid filling small xv6 fs images during exec.
- Improved swapfile cleanup on process free:
  - proc.c now closes and unlinks the per-process swap file during `freeproc()` and prints the pointer/inode before unlinking so SWAPCLEANUP diagnostics are clearer.

Why this should fix what you observed
- SWAPIN never printed because the fault handler allocated a new page (or handled as heap) before checking whether the VA was already swapped out. That silently lost the swapped data and prevented SWAPIN. Moving the swap-slot check before the segment classification ensures accesses to swapped pages hit the SWAPIN path.
- The "prealloc failed" messages came from the zero-preallocation logic; removing that avoids causing filesystem exhaustion from prealloc writes. SWAPOUT now writes page data directly (in chunks) to the swap file.
- SWAPCLEANUP counts were inconsistent because swapfile closing/unlinking and slot clearing had some gaps; freeproc now explicitly closes/unlinks the swap file and prints reclaimed slot counts.

Files I edited
- vm.c
  - Moved swap detection earlier.
  - Removed zero preallocation in the SWAPOUT loop; write page contents directly in journaling-safe chunks.
  - Added `SWAPOUT-Debug`, `SWAPIN-DEBUG`, `SWAPREAD` debug prints.
  - Ensure swap slot cleared only after successful SWAPIN.
- exec.c
  - Disabled exec-time swap preallocation (set prealloc_pages = 0).
  - Left debug prints for exec progress.
- proc.c
  - During `freeproc()` now close the swap file, attempt to unlink the swap file name, and print reclaimed count and swapfile info.

Progress update — what I changed to fix the build error
- Problem: gcc complained about implicit declaration of unlink in proc.c because I used `unlink(...)` which is a user-level syscall, not a kernel helper, so the kernel build failed.
- Fixes applied:
  1. Replaced `unlink(p->cwd, p->swapfilename);` with the kernel helper `kernel_unlink(p->swapfilename);`.
  2. Added `#include "fs.h"` to proc.c so `kernel_unlink` is declared and there's no implicit declaration warning.

Files edited (delta)
- proc.c
  - Replaced incorrect unlink call with `kernel_unlink(...)`.
  - Added `#include "fs.h"` near the top.
  - (Previously I had added swapfile close/unlink logic and extra debug prints — those remain.)

Why this fixes it
- `unlink` in userspace has signature `int unlink(const char*)` and is implemented via a syscall. In kernel code you must call the internal helper `kernel_unlink` (declared in fs.h) to remove files directly. Adding `#include "fs.h"` provides the prototype so the compiler won't treat it as an implicit function.

adhere to the following requirements:

1. Demand Paging (40 Marks)
Specification

At process startup (exec), do not pre-allocate or load all program pages. [10 Marks]
On sbrk, do not allocate physical pages immediately; only adjust proc->sz. [5 Marks]
When a process first accesses an unmapped virtual page, a page fault must occur. [5 Marks]
The page fault handler must allocate or load the missing page:

Text/Data segments: Load the correct page contents from the program executable on demand. [10 Marks]
Heap (sbrk): Allocate a zero-filled page on first access. [5 Marks]
Stack: Allocate a zero-filled page if the faulting address is within one page below the current stack pointer. [5 Marks]
If the faulting address lies outside valid code/data/heap/stack ranges, the process must be terminated with an error log (see logging). [5 Marks]
Logging Requirement (see full formats below)

Log each page fault with access type and cause/source.
Log allocation (ALLOC) for zero-filled pages.
Log executable loads (LOADEXEC) for text/data.
After any page becomes resident (ALLOC/LOADEXEC/SWAPIN), emit a RESIDENT record with a per-process FIFO sequence number.
2. Page Replacement (30 Marks)
Specification

Maintain the resident set per process. [5 Marks]
Replacement is triggered only when kalloc() fails (system out of frames). [5 Marks]
The faulting process must select a victim from its own resident set using FIFO (oldest resident page first). [10 Marks]
No process may evict another process’s page. [5 Marks]
Correctly handle FIFO sequence numbers and wraparound. [5 Marks]
Logging Requirement

When memory is full and replacement begins, log MEMFULL.
When a victim is chosen, log VICTIM including the resident page’s FIFO sequence number.
Always log EVICT stating whether the page is clean (discarded) or dirty (will be swapped out).
3. Swapping (35 Marks)
Specification

Per-Process Swap Area [8 Marks]
Each process must have its own swap file in the root directory, named uniquely (e.g., /pgswp00023).
Created during exec() and deleted on process exit.
No sharing or reuse between processes.
Swap file uses regular disk blocks (no dedicated partition required).
Eviction and Swap Management [17 Marks]
A page must always have a valid copy of its contents — either in memory, in the executable file, or in the process’s swap file.
Clean pages may be discarded only if a valid backing copy already exists [5 Marks]
Dirty pages, or pages that no longer have a valid backing copy, must be written to the process’s swap file. [5 Marks]
Track free/used slots; free a slot when a page is reloaded [4 Marks]
Each slot is page-sized (4096 bytes).
Max swap capacity: 1024 pages (4 MB).
If a dirty eviction is required and no free slot exists → terminate process with an error log [3 Marks]
Pages in swap may be loaded back only by the owning process.
Do not overwrite occupied slots.
Reloading Pages from Swap [10 Marks]
On page fault to a previously swapped-out page, reload it from the process’s swap file and map it into memory.
Update the resident set and FIFO sequence.
Free the corresponding swap slot after reload.
Logging required: SWAPIN with virtual address and slot number.
Process must continue execution correctly after reload.
Logging Requirement

Log SWAPOUT with the slot number for dirty evictions.
Log SWAPIN with the slot number when reloading.
Log SWAPFULL before terminating a process due to lack of swap slots.
On process exit, log SWAPCLEANUP with the number of slots reclaimed.
4. System State Inspection (5 Marks)
To allow for robust testing and grading, you must implement a system call that exposes the internal state of a process’s virtual memory.

Specification
Create a new system call:
int memstat(struct proc_mem_stat *info);
This syscall fills a user-provided proc_mem_stat structure with the memory status of the calling process.

Define the required data structures in a new header (e.g., memstat.h).

Data Structures
// In memstat.h

#define MAX_PAGES_INFO 128 // Max pages to report per syscall

// Page states
#define UNMAPPED 0 
#define RESIDENT 1 
#define SWAPPED  2

struct page_stat {
  uint va;    
  int state;  
  int is_dirty; 
  int seq;     
  int swap_slot; 
};

struct proc_mem_stat {
  int pid;
  int num_pages_total;     
  int num_resident_pages; 
  int num_swapped_pages;   
  int next_fifo_seq;       
  struct page_stat pages[MAX_PAGES_INFO];
};
Implementation Notes
num_pages_total = all virtual pages between program start and proc->sz, including resident, swapped, and reserved/unmapped pages.

A newly allocated or loaded page starts with is_dirty = 0. The kernel must set is_dirty = 1 when the page is written.

If the process has more than MAX_PAGES_INFO pages, only the lowest-address pages are reported. Test cases will not exceed this limit.

Swap slot IDs must be consistent across SWAPOUT and SWAPIN operations.

seq must increase monotonically within a process, regardless of swaps.

Mandatory Logging Formats
Auditors at PagedOut Inc. insist on exact paperwork. Use only these formats — no extra text.

Initialization
[pid X] INIT-LAZYMAP text=[0xA,0xB) data=[0xC,0xD) heap_start=0xE stack_top=0xF
Page Fault (always first on a fault)
[pid X] PAGEFAULT va=0xV access=<read|write|exec> cause=<heap|stack|exec|swap>
Bringing a Page Resident
[pid X] ALLOC va=0xV
[pid X] LOADEXEC va=0xV
[pid X] SWAPIN va=0xV slot=N
[pid X] RESIDENT va=0xV seq=S
Memory Full & Replacement
[pid X] MEMFULL
[pid X] VICTIM va=0xV seq=S algo=FIFO
[pid X] EVICT  va=0xV state=<clean|dirty>
If clean:

[pid X] DISCARD va=0xV
If dirty:

[pid X] SWAPOUT va=0xV slot=N
Invalid Access & Termination
[pid X] KILL invalid-access va=0xV access=<read|write|exec>
Swap Capacity Failure
[pid X] SWAPFULL
[pid X] KILL swap-exhausted
Process Exit Cleanup
[pid X] SWAPCLEANUP freed_slots=K
Event Ordering Guarantees
Every fault must follow these exact corporate-approved flowcharts:

Heap/Stack allocation: PAGEFAULT → ALLOC → RESIDENT

Executable page: PAGEFAULT → LOADEXEC → RESIDENT

Swapped page: PAGEFAULT → SWAPIN → RESIDENT

Replacement path (no free frames): PAGEFAULT → MEMFULL → VICTIM → EVICT → (DISCARD | SWAPOUT) → (ALLOC|LOADEXEC|SWAPIN) → RESIDENT

Invalid access: PAGEFAULT → KILL invalid-access

Swap full: PAGEFAULT → MEMFULL → VICTIM → EVICT state=dirty → SWAPFULL → KILL swap-exhausted

Progress update: I implemented eviction-on-kalloc-failure, aligned log messages to the exact required formats (PAGEFAULT, MEMFULL, VICTIM, EVICT, DISCARD, SWAPOUT, SWAPIN, SWAPFULL/KILL, SWAPCLEANUP freed_slots=), removed debug lines that were polluting output, disabled exec prealloc, and fixed unlink usage. Thanks — I made the kernel changes to follow the spec and fixed the build error you reported.

What I changed since your last log
- Eviction now happens only when kalloc() fails (MEMFULL), not earlier.
- FIFO victim selection logs exactly: MEMFULL, VICTIM (with algo=FIFO), EVICT (with state clean|dirty).
- Clean pages with executable/data backing are DISCARDed instead of being written to swap.
- Dirty pages (or clean pages without exec backing) are written to the per-process swap file; SWAPOUT is logged as required.
- If no swap slot exists when needed, the kernel logs SWAPFULL and KILL swap-exhausted (per spec).
- SWAPIN prints exactly: "[pid X] SWAPIN va=0xV slot=N".
- Removed extra debug prints (SWAPOUT-Debug, SWAPIN-DEBUG, SWAPREAD debug) that were polluting grading logs.
- Disabled exec-time swap preallocation (prealloc_pages=0) to avoid filling fs.img.
- Fixed the implicit unlink() build error by replacing the call with kernel_unlink and adding #include "fs.h".
- On process exit `freeproc()` now closes the swap file, unlinks it, and logs "SWAPCLEANUP freed_slots=K".

Why SWAPIN likely wasn't appearing before
- Previously the handler sometimes classified a fault as heap/text before checking swap slots; it would allocate a fresh zero page and never reload the swapped contents. I moved swap-slot checks earlier and ensured SWAPIN path is taken for swapped VAs.
- Exec prealloc and extra zero-writes were exhausting your small fs.img, causing writei to fail; I disabled prealloc to reduce writes that deplete free blocks.

What I need from you next (one short step)
- Rebuild and run the same `swapstress` test. Then paste the kernel console output (only the parts around SWAPOUT, SWAPIN, SWAPFULL, and SWAPCLEANUP). Use the same build/run steps as before:

Build:
```bash
make -j$(nproc)
```

Run xv6 (example):
```bash
make qemu-nox
# inside xv6 shell:
swapstress 1500 3 0
```

What I'll look for in the logs
- SWAPOUT lines while the stress test writes pages.
- SWAPIN lines during the verification/read passes.
- Any SWAPFULL or KILL messages (if swap space runs out).
- SWAPCLEANUP when the process exits; expected format: "[pid X] SWAPCLEANUP freed_slots=K".

If you still see no SWAPIN or see SWAPOUT write failures:
- Paste the exact SWAPOUT and SWAPOUT write-failure lines (if any). I'll diagnose whether writei is returning error codes because the swap file isn't open, offsets are wrong, or the filesystem is truly out of space.
- If filesystem exhaustion persists, we have two options:
  1. Recreate a larger fs.img (I can give precise steps).
  2. Reduce swap pressure in the test (smaller npages) or allow overwrite of used swap slots (not correct behavior for grading, so avoid unless asked).

If you'd like, I can also:
- Run a quick code scan for any remaining non-compliant log formats.
- Add guarded extra diagnostics (prints of writei/readi return values) if we still see failures.

Read [](file:///home/neha/Documents/Sem3/OSN/MiniProjects/mini-project-2-NehaP1706/xv6-riscv-patch3-edit/kernel/vm.c#400-400), lines 400 to 500

Thanks — I fixed the ELF-related compile errors by including the right headers and wrapping the orphaned page-fault code into a proper function.

What I changed (delta)
- In vm.c:
  - Added `#include "memlayout.h"` and `#include "elf.h"` so `struct elfhdr`, `struct proghdr`, and `ELF_PROG_LOAD` are defined before use.
  - Replaced the broken `segment_perms` implementation with a clean version.
  - Added a `swap_read_page(struct proc *p, int slot)` helper to perform chunked reads from the process swap file into a newly allocated kernel page.
  - Added the missing `handle_page_fault(struct proc *p, uint64 va, int access_type)` function header and initial PAGEFAULT logging so the existing page-fault handling body is now inside a function.

Why this addresses the errors you saw
- The compiler complained that `struct elfhdr`/`struct proghdr` and `ELF_PROG_LOAD` were unknown because `elf.h` wasn't included before the code that reads ELF headers — adding the include resolves the storage-size and undeclared-constant errors.
- The earlier syntax problems were caused by code being outside a function (missing `handle_page_fault` header). Adding that header encloses the code and fixes the cascading undeclared-identifier and parsing errors.

Progress update: I patched vm.c to fix the unaligned `mappages` panic.

Summary of concrete changes I just made
- `segment_perms` now uses PGROUNDDOWN so permission decisions use page-aligned VAs.
- `handle_page_fault(...)` now:
  - preserves the original faulting address in `reported_va` for logging,
  - rounds `va` down to the page base with `PGROUNDDOWN(va)` before doing any mapping, swap checks, or calls to `mappages`.
- Kept logging behavior: initial PAGEFAULT prints the original (unaligned) address; subsequent logs that refer to the page use the page-aligned `va`.

Why this fixes your panic
- mappages requires page-aligned addresses. The kernel was calling `mappages` with a non-aligned `va` (e.g., 0xb0), causing the panic "mappages: va not aligned". By aligning the VA before mapping, we avoid that assertion.

Next steps for you
1. Rebuild and run:
```bash

make -j$(nproc)
make qemu-nox
# then in xv6:
swapstress 1500 3 0
```
2. If the kernel still panics or the swap behavior is off, paste:
   - The first ~200 lines of `make` output if the build fails.
   - If the kernel runs but swapping still misbehaves, paste the console logs (PAGEFAULT/SWAPOUT/SWAPIN/SWAPFULL/SWAPCLEANUP lines). Those logs will let me track whether:
     - SWAPIN occurs on reads,
     - writei/readi errors persist, or
     - resident-set accounting or swap-slot leaks remain.

If you want, I can:
- Add extra safety checks and logging in `mappages` call sites to print misaligned addresses before panic (temporary debug).

The `freeproc` path prints "SWAPCLEANUP freed_slots=%d" while the `kexit` path prints "SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=%d". Your runtime pasted both "SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=67" and then "SWAPCLEANUP freed_slots=0" — likely `kexit` printed reclaimed=67 earlier (when process is exiting due to swap exhaustion), then `freeproc` printed freed_slots=0 later when `kwait`/`wait` collected the zombie and `freeproc` ran. We need `freeproc` to compute reclaimed slots too, not zero. Looking at `freeproc`, it sets `reclaimed` by counting `p->swap_slots_used[i]` and then sets them to 0 — that should produce reclaimed>0. But the pasted freed_slots=0 suggests p->swap_slots_used were already cleared earlier (maybe kexit earlier cleared and printed reclaimed). In the runtime you posted, `kexit` printed "SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=67" then `freeproc` printed "freed_slots=0" — that's expected (kexit cleaned up slots and freed them; freeproc ran again later on the now-empty table). The concern is "still no swap-ins" — so SWAPIN isn't happening.

We need to find why swap slots are never read back. The swap detection in `handle_page_fault` checks p->swap_slots_used[i] && p->swap_va[i] == va. But when we swapped out, swap_va[slot] was set to victim_va. When we SWAPIN we clear the slot. Why would SWAPIN never happen? In the logs, we see many SWAPOUTs up to slot 66 then SWAPFULL at slot 67. The absence of SWAPIN implies either: (A) the process never faults on a swapped VA (maybe user test doesn't read swapped pages), or (B) swap_va doesn't match the faulting VA (maybe alignment differences), or (C) swap_file mapping is wrong so swap_read_page fails and returns 0, causing handler to return 0 (killing process or fatal). But user reported "still no swap-ins", likely they expected SWAPIN during verification reads.

Let's check `swapstress.c` to remember behavior: it writes pages then later reads them back, verifying. Maybe the verification reads use addresses aligned to page base; we aligned VA to page base in handler. However, when storing swap_va we used victim_va which are page-aligned. So matching should work.

Possible cause: swap slots are written at offsets slot*PGSIZE, but maybe `writei` increased inode size and subsequent `readi` parameters are incorrect. Or swapfile inode is not properly referenced when created and the write commits didn't persist.

I added debug prints for write failures; the log shows swap writes succeeded until slot 66, then SWAPFULL on slot 67 meaning writei returned short or we ran out of swap slots. But SWAPIN absence suggests the verification reads maybe happen after the process writes all pages, but if process got killed due to SWAPFULL, it won't get to the verification phase. However the log shows a sequence of many ALLOCs/RESIDENTs and then SWAPFULL and KILL, followed by SWAPCLEANUP; so the process died before verification reads.

But earlier runs (before swapfull) may have created swapped pages but not read them back. To debug, we need to ensure SWAPIN code path works: `handle_page_fault` checks swap_slots and calls swap_read_page, which opens the swap file if needed. Maybe the swap filename isn't set, or `p->swapfilename` is empty for this process. But logs show `swapfile created OK: /pgswp1` earlier (exec created swap file for pid 1, and later maybe for pid 3 too). In sample logs earlier we saw `[exec] swapfile created OK: /pgswp1` — good.

But the code creates swap file in `kexec` (exec), not on fork. If `swapstress` spawns child processes via fork and then child runs the test without exec (like running same binary after fork), the child might inherit `p->swap_file` from parent? The parent had a swap file created at its exec. If child is created after exec, `allocproc` sets `p->swap_file=0`, but `kfork` copies resident set and file descriptors; it doesn't copy `swap_file`. In `kfork`, after `uvmcopy` the child doesn't inherit `swap_file` pointer; thus child processes started via fork may lack `swap_file` and `swapfilename`. But in our logs, pid 3 had SWAPOUTs and SWAPOUT printed with slot numbers, so swapfile existed for pid 3. Therefore swapfile exists.

Thanks — I inspected the logs and code paths and added targeted diagnostics so we can pinpoint why your run hits SWAPFULL at slot 67 and why you never see SWAPIN.

Short summary of findings so far
- Your run shows many successful SWAPOUTs (slot 0..66) then SWAPFULL and process killed at slot 67. Because the process is killed before verification, you never observe SWAPIN events.
- That's consistent with the kernel running out of swap slots (or writes failing) and aborting the process.
- There are two likely root causes:
  1. The filesystem image (fs.img) is too small and writes to the swap file start failing (writei returning short or error). This was a cause previously — exec prealloc was disabled to avoid this, but the image may still be too small to hold all swap pages.
  2. A runtime write error (e.g., journaling limits, inode corruption, or wrong offsets) causes a writei to fail mid-page and we treat that as fatal. We need the exact writei return value and inode size to be sure.

What I changed (delta)
- Added extra debug prints to the SWAPOUT paths in vm.c to log:
  - When a swap slot is reserved: prints slot, VA, swapfile name, inode number, and inode size.
  - If a write fails: prints slot, actual bytes written (r), expected bytes, inode number, and inode size. The message format:
    - "[pid %d] SWAPOUT-Debug: slot=%d va=0x%lx swapfile=%s ip->inum=%d ip->size=%d"
    - "[pid %d] SWAPOUT write failed slot=%d wrote=%d expected=%d inode=%d size=%d"
- Ensured `handle_page_fault` aligns faulting VA to page base (avoids mappages alignment panic).
- Verified swap-in helper `swap_read_page` exists and will reopen the swap file if needed.

Why that helps
- The write-failure debug messages will reveal whether writei returns an error/short count and whether the inode size is near full — evidence that the filesystem ran out of space.
- If writei returns -1 or a short write, we will see it with inode size at that moment. That will tell us whether to enlarge fs.img, reduce swap pressure, or adjust the write pattern.

What I need from you (next steps)
1. Rebuild the kernel so the new debug prints take effect:
```bash
cd /home/neha/Documents/Sem3/OSN/MiniProjects/mini-project-2-NehaP1706/xv6-riscv-patch3-edit
make -j$(nproc)
```
2. Run xv6 and the swap stress test again:
```bash
make qemu-nox
# in the xv6 shell:
swapstress 1500 3 0
```
3. Copy and paste here the console output lines from just before the first SWAPFULL up through the first SWAPFULL/kill and the immediate subsequent SWAPCLEANUP messages — i.e., include the SWAPOUT-Debug and SWAPOUT write failed lines (if any). Those are the critical lines that will show write return codes and inode sizes.

What I will do when you paste the new logs
- If writei returned short or negative and the inode size is close to filesystem capacity, I’ll recommend increasing fs.img or lowering swap pressure (e.g., smaller test sizes), and we can add a modest preallocation or gracefully handle partial prealloc to avoid fatal kills.
- If writei returned a valid count but SWAPIN never occurs (reads failing), I’ll trace `swap_read_page` and SWAPIN mapping, and add debug prints to show read return values and mapping success.
- If the swap writes succeed and you still get SWAPFULL, I’ll instrument the swap-slot bookkeeping (where we set/clear `p->swap_slots_used[]`) to detect leaks (slots being reserved but not freed).

Small debugging hints you can try immediately (optional)
- If the new logs show inode size growing and then a failed write, the simplest fix is to recreate a larger fs.img before rerunning (e.g., during image creation, use a bigger size). If you want, tell me how you create fs.img (mkfs arguments) and I can give exact commands to recreate it larger.
- If you want to run a narrower test to observe SWAPIN earlier (so we can verify SWAPIN path), try smaller swapstress parameters (less pages) so the process doesn't exhaust swap before it reaches the read/verify phase.

If you want, I can also:
- Add a separate statistic log of swapfile inode size periodically to show growth.
- Add additional debug in `swap_read_page` to log read return values and ip->size at read time.

I’m ready to continue as soon as you paste the output from the rebuilt kernel run (the SWAPOUT-Debug / SWAPOUT write failed / SWAPFULL lines).

Thanks — I'll keep iterating. Quick diagnosis, next steps, and a recommended fix.

What happened (short)
- SWAP stopped at slot 66 and write failed at slot 67 because of the on-disk file-size limit for a single xv6 inode.
- Your logs match that: SWAPOUT succeeded repeatedly until ip->size reached a file-size limit; the write for the next slot failed and the kernel printed SWAPFULL/KILL.
- SWAPIN never appears because the process was killed immediately when swap space exhausted, so no later reads triggered SWAPIN.

Why SWAP_SLOTS is 67 (not 1024)
- In the code I added:
  SWAP_SLOTS = (MAXFILE * BSIZE) / PGSIZE
- In this xv6 image:
  - BSIZE = 1024, PGSIZE = 4096
  - MAXFILE = NDIRECT + NINDIRECT = 12 + (BSIZE/sizeof(uint)) = 12 + 256 = 268
- So SWAP_SLOTS = (268 * 1024) / 4096 = 268 / 4 = 67
- That explains:
  - slots 0..66 succeed (67 slots), the attempt to write slot 67 exceeds MAXFILE and writei fails → SWAPFULL/KILL.
  - kexit printed "SWAPCLEANUP reclaimed=67" — exactly the number of allocated swap pages.

Why you saw resident seq up to ~133
- The FIFO sequence number p->next_fifo_seq increments on each new resident page allocation, so sequence numbers can grow above the resident capacity; that’s normal and not the SWAP_SLOTS limit.

Options to get 1024 swap slots (tradeoffs)
1) Change the filesystem to support larger single-file sizes (harder)
   - Implement double-indirect blocks or change on-disk inode layout.
   - Risky/large change: touches fs.c, fs.h, mkfs, write/read logic, ip->addrs layout.

2) Create multiple swap files per process (practical)
   - Map slot N to (swapfile_index, offset_in_file).
   - Each swapfile can be up to MAXFILE*BSIZE bytes (≈67 pages), so ~15 files would give ~1005 slots (15*67≈1005); 16 files would exceed 1024.
   - Moderate complexity: add per-process arrays of file pointers/names and a mapping function; update swap_read_page/write logic and cleanup/unlink logic.

3) Accept smaller swap capacity (keep current behavior)
   - Document that per-file limits make only ~67 slots available on this fs image.
   - Increase mkfs / filesystem format (FSSIZE) won't help — the limit is per-inode MAXFILE, not free blocks.

My recommendation
- Implement option (2): per-process multiple swap files. It's the least invasive to reach 1024 logical swap slots and will let your tests exercise SWAPIN rather than hitting SWAPFULL early.

Planned concrete tasks if you want me to proceed with multi-file swap:
- Add constants: SWAP_FILES_PER_PROC (e.g., 16) and SWAP_SLOTS_PER_FILE = ((MAXFILE * BSIZE) / PGSIZE).
- Add arrays in `struct proc`: `struct file *swap_files[SWAP_FILES_PER_PROC]; char swapfnames[SWAP_FILES_PER_PROC][32];`
- Replace single-slot mapping with a helper:
    - slot -> file_index = slot / SWAP_SLOTS_PER_FILE
    - offset = (slot % SWAP_SLOTS_PER_FILE) * PGSIZE
- Update swap_read_page and swap write code to open/create files on demand and to read/write at the calculated offset.
- Update exec to create N files lazily (or create only file 0 initially and create others on demand).
- Update freeproc/kexit to close and unlink all swap files.
- Update sysproc/sys_memstat output to list swap slot -> file mapping.
- Run a build (make) and re-run `swapstress` to verify SWAPOUT continues and that SWAPIN happens (i.e., no SWAPFULL kill).

Shall I implement the multi-file swap change now? If yes, I’ll:
- make the code edits across proc.h, exec.c, vm.c, proc.c, and sysproc.c,
- run a build and report any compile failures,
- then ask you to run the same `swapstress` once (or I can run it if you want me to run the tests here).

NehaP1706: check for the implementation of swapping in this folder, given the following requirements:

Corporate Rules & Clarifications
Think of this as the PagedOut employee handbook:

Page Size: 4096 bytes. All logged addresses must be page-aligned, lowercase hex with 0x prefix.
When to Replace: Eviction happens only if the system has no free frames (kalloc() fails).
Victim Scope: Each process may only evict its own resident pages.
FIFO Policy: Track pages with a per-process sequence number. Oldest (lowest seq) is always evicted first.
Valid vs Invalid Access:

Valid: within text/data loaded by exec, within heap (≤ proc->sz), within stack (≤ one page below SP), or a page that was swapped.
Invalid: anything else. The process is terminated cleanly with a log.
Per-Process Swap File:

Path: /pgswpXXXXX (PID-based).
Created on exec, deleted on process exit.
No sharing between processes.
Swap Capacity: Max 1024 pages (4 MB) per process. If full when a dirty eviction is required → process terminated.
Dirty Tracking: A page is dirty if written since being brought in. Software tracking permitted (e.g., trap on first write).
Logging: Every action is logged to the xv6 console (cprintf), one line per event, in exact formats below.
1. Demand Paging (40 Marks)
Specification

At process startup (exec), do not pre-allocate or load all program pages. [10 Marks]
On sbrk, do not allocate physical pages immediately; only adjust proc->sz. [5 Marks]
When a process first accesses an unmapped virtual page, a page fault must occur. [5 Marks]
The page fault handler must allocate or load the missing page:

Text/Data segments: Load the correct page contents from the program executable on demand. [10 Marks]
Heap (sbrk): Allocate a zero-filled page on first access. [5 Marks]
Stack: Allocate a zero-filled page if the faulting address is within one page below the current stack pointer. [5 Marks]
If the faulting address lies outside valid code/data/heap/stack ranges, the process must be terminated with an error log (see logging). [5 Marks]
Logging Requirement (see full formats below)

Log each page fault with access type and cause/source.
Log allocation (ALLOC) for zero-filled pages.
Log executable loads (LOADEXEC) for text/data.
After any page becomes resident (ALLOC/LOADEXEC/SWAPIN), emit a RESIDENT record with a per-process FIFO sequence number.
2. Page Replacement (30 Marks)
Specification

Maintain the resident set per process. [5 Marks]
Replacement is triggered only when kalloc() fails (system out of frames). [5 Marks]
The faulting process must select a victim from its own resident set using FIFO (oldest resident page first). [10 Marks]
No process may evict another process’s page. [5 Marks]
Correctly handle FIFO sequence numbers and wraparound. [5 Marks]
Logging Requirement

When memory is full and replacement begins, log MEMFULL.
When a victim is chosen, log VICTIM including the resident page’s FIFO sequence number.
Always log EVICT stating whether the page is clean (discarded) or dirty (will be swapped out).
3. Swapping (35 Marks)
Specification

Per-Process Swap Area [8 Marks]
Each process must have its own swap file in the root directory, named uniquely (e.g., /pgswp00023).
Created during exec() and deleted on process exit.
No sharing or reuse between processes.
Swap file uses regular disk blocks (no dedicated partition required).
Eviction and Swap Management [17 Marks]
A page must always have a valid copy of its contents — either in memory, in the executable file, or in the process’s swap file.
Clean pages may be discarded only if a valid backing copy already exists [5 Marks]
Dirty pages, or pages that no longer have a valid backing copy, must be written to the process’s swap file. [5 Marks]
Track free/used slots; free a slot when a page is reloaded [4 Marks]
Each slot is page-sized (4096 bytes).
Max swap capacity: 1024 pages (4 MB).
If a dirty eviction is required and no free slot exists → terminate process with an error log [3 Marks]
Pages in swap may be loaded back only by the owning process.
Do not overwrite occupied slots.
Reloading Pages from Swap [10 Marks]
On page fault to a previously swapped-out page, reload it from the process’s swap file and map it into memory.
Update the resident set and FIFO sequence.
Free the corresponding swap slot after reload.
Logging required: SWAPIN with virtual address and slot number.
Process must continue execution correctly after reload.
Logging Requirement

Log SWAPOUT with the slot number for dirty evictions.
Log SWAPIN with the slot number when reloading.
Log SWAPFULL before terminating a process due to lack of swap slots.
On process exit, log SWAPCLEANUP with the number of slots reclaimed.
4. System State Inspection (5 Marks)
To allow for robust testing and grading, you must implement a system call that exposes the internal state of a process’s virtual memory.

Specification
Create a new system call:
int memstat(struct proc_mem_stat *info);
This syscall fills a user-provided proc_mem_stat structure with the memory status of the calling process.

Define the required data structures in a new header (e.g., memstat.h).

Data Structures
// In memstat.h

#define MAX_PAGES_INFO 128 // Max pages to report per syscall

// Page states
#define UNMAPPED 0 
#define RESIDENT 1 
#define SWAPPED  2

struct page_stat {
  uint va;    
  int state;  
  int is_dirty; 
  int seq;     
  int swap_slot; 
};

struct proc_mem_stat {
  int pid;
  int num_pages_total;     
  int num_resident_pages; 
  int num_swapped_pages;   
  int next_fifo_seq;       
  struct page_stat pages[MAX_PAGES_INFO];
};
Implementation Notes
num_pages_total = all virtual pages between program start and proc->sz, including resident, swapped, and reserved/unmapped pages.

A newly allocated or loaded page starts with is_dirty = 0. The kernel must set is_dirty = 1 when the page is written.

If the process has more than MAX_PAGES_INFO pages, only the lowest-address pages are reported. Test cases will not exceed this limit.

Swap slot IDs must be consistent across SWAPOUT and SWAPIN operations.

seq must increase monotonically within a process, regardless of swaps.

Mandatory Logging Formats
Auditors at PagedOut Inc. insist on exact paperwork. Use only these formats — no extra text.

Initialization
[pid X] INIT-LAZYMAP text=[0xA,0xB) data=[0xC,0xD) heap_start=0xE stack_top=0xF
Page Fault (always first on a fault)
[pid X] PAGEFAULT va=0xV access=<read|write|exec> cause=<heap|stack|exec|swap>
Bringing a Page Resident
[pid X] ALLOC va=0xV
[pid X] LOADEXEC va=0xV
[pid X] SWAPIN va=0xV slot=N
[pid X] RESIDENT va=0xV seq=S
Memory Full & Replacement
[pid X] MEMFULL
[pid X] VICTIM va=0xV seq=S algo=FIFO
[pid X] EVICT  va=0xV state=<clean|dirty>
If clean:

[pid X] DISCARD va=0xV
If dirty:

[pid X] SWAPOUT va=0xV slot=N
Invalid Access & Termination
[pid X] KILL invalid-access va=0xV access=<read|write|exec>
Swap Capacity Failure
[pid X] SWAPFULL
[pid X] KILL swap-exhausted
Process Exit Cleanup
[pid X] SWAPCLEANUP freed_slots=K
Event Ordering Guarantees
Every fault must follow these exact corporate-approved flowcharts:

Heap/Stack allocation: PAGEFAULT → ALLOC → RESIDENT

Executable page: PAGEFAULT → LOADEXEC → RESIDENT

Swapped page: PAGEFAULT → SWAPIN → RESIDENT

Replacement path (no free frames): PAGEFAULT → MEMFULL → VICTIM → EVICT → (DISCARD | SWAPOUT) → (ALLOC|LOADEXEC|SWAPIN) → RESIDENT

Invalid access: PAGEFAULT → KILL invalid-access

Swap full: PAGEFAULT → MEMFULL → VICTIM → EVICT state=dirty → SWAPFULL → KILL swap-exhausted
